(3) *Switching attention*: flexibly shifting between task sets, retrieval strategies, or mental representations (with associated switching costs). (4) *Interfacing with long-term memory*: selectively activating relevant long-term knowledge to support current working memory operations, enabling reasoning and comprehension tasks that require integration of stored knowledge with new information.

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[baddeley-model-of-working-memory-foundational-report-2026-04-03]])*
> **Q:** Application: Modality Effect in Multimedia Learning
> **A:** **Q:** What is the modality effect in multimedia learning, how does Baddeley's model explain it, and what is its design implication?
> **A:** The modality effect: learning is better when complex visual material is accompanied by spoken narration rather than on-screen text. Baddeley's explanation: text and diagram compete for the same visuospatial processing channel, causing bottleneck. Narration and diagram use separate channels (phonological loop for narration; visuospatial sketchpad for diagram), allowing parallel processing and more total information capacity. Design implication: for complex animated or diagrammatic material, use spoken narration rather than on-screen explanatory text to exploit the dual-channel architecture of working memory.

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[chunking-and-expertise-development-foundational-report-2026-04-15]])*
> **Q:** Definition: Chunking
> **A:** **Q:** What is chunking in cognitive psychology, and what problem does it solve?
> **A:** Chunking is the cognitive process of recoding multiple individual information elements into a single integrated unit (a chunk) that occupies one slot in working memory. It solves the problem of working memory's severe capacity limitation (~4 items) by compressing the format of representation so that more information can be processed within the same narrow bandwidth.
> **Source:** Section I
> **Difficulty:** Basic
> **Tags:** #chunking #working-memory #definition

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[chunking-and-expertise-development-foundational-report-2026-04-15]])*
> **Q:** Distinction: Chunks vs. Schemas
> **A:** **Q:** How do chunks differ from schemas, and what is their developmental relationship?
> **A:** Chunks are specific, relatively concrete patterns bound by meaningful relations (e.g., a chess piece configuration). Schemas are more abstract organizational frameworks that govern the interpretation of entire categories of situations (e.g., the schema for "kingside attack"). The developmental relationship is constructive: chunks are the building blocks from which schemas are assembled through practice and experience.
> **Source:** Section II
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** #chunking #schema-theory #distinction

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[chunking-and-expertise-development-foundational-report-2026-04-15]])*
> **Q:** Process: Schema Automation and Bandwidth Liberation
> **A:** **Q:** What happens when a schema becomes automated, and why is this significant for expertise development?
> **A:** When a schema becomes automated, it can operate without consuming working memory resources — executing upon activation without conscious control. This frees working memory capacity for higher-order cognitive tasks (strategic planning, creative thinking, further learning), creating a self-reinforcing cycle: each increment of automation opens cognitive space for the next increment of schema construction. This is the engine of progressive expertise development.
> **Source:** Section IV
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** #schema-automation #cognitive-load #expertise-development

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[chunking-and-expertise-development-foundational-report-2026-04-15]])*
> **Q:** Key Finding: Chi et al. Expert-Novice Study
> **A:** **Q:** What did Chi, Feltovich, and Glaser (1981) discover about how experts and novices categorize physics problems, and what does this reveal about expertise?
> **A:** Novices categorized physics problems by surface features (inclined plane vs. spring problems) while experts categorized by deep principles (conservation of energy vs. Newton's second law). This demonstrated that expertise involves the *reorganization* of knowledge around functional principles, not merely the accumulation of more information — a direct product of chunk formation at the conceptual level.
> **Source:** Section III
> **Difficulty:** Basic
> **Tags:** #expert-novice #chunking #physics

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[chunking-and-expertise-development-foundational-report-2026-04-15]])*
> **Q:** Concept: Expertise Reversal Effect
> **A:** **Q:** What is the expertise reversal effect, and what causes it?
> **A:** The expertise reversal effect occurs when instructional techniques effective for novices become ineffective or detrimental for more advanced learners (and vice versa). It is caused by the interaction between instruction and the learner's developing schema library: instruction that scaffolds schema construction for novices becomes redundant with experts' automated schemas, imposing extraneous cognitive load instead of reducing it.
> **Source:** Section VII
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** #expertise-reversal-effect #cognitive-load-theory #instructional-design

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[chunking-and-expertise-development-foundational-report-2026-04-15]])*
> **Q:** Application: Deliberate Practice Requirements
> **A:** **Q:** What five characteristics distinguish deliberate practice from routine experience?
> **A:** (1) Well-defined performance goals targeting specific weaknesses; (2) Full concentration and effort — not enjoyable or automatic; (3) Immediate, informative feedback allowing error detection and correction; (4) Iterative refinement through repetition with progressive correction; (5) Operation at the boundary of current ability — challenging enough to require effort but not so difficult as to preclude progress.
> **Source:** Section VI
> **Difficulty:** Basic
> **Tags:** #deliberate-practice #expertise-development

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[chunking-and-expertise-development-foundational-report-2026-04-15]])*
> **Q:** Connection: RPD and Chunking
> **A:** **Q:** How does the Recognition-Primed Decision model explain expert intuition in terms of chunking?
> **A:** RPD proposes that expert decisions arise through rapid pattern recognition rather than systematic option comparison. The expert assesses the situation, recognizes it as an instance of a familiar type (activating a stored chunk), and thereby retrieves a workable response. Each chunk encodes not just a perceptual pattern but an integrated package of goals, cues, expectations, and appropriate actions — producing the appearance of intuitive insight through automated chunk retrieval.
> **Source:** Section V
> **Difficulty:** Advanced
> **Tags:** #rpd #expert-intuition #pattern-recognition

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[chunking-and-expertise-development-foundational-report-2026-04-15]])*
> **Q:** Original Synthesis: Currency Hypothesis
> **A:** **Q:** What is the "currency hypothesis" proposed in this report, and what does it explain?
> **A:** The currency hypothesis proposes that chunking is the *unit currency* of deliberate practice — the fundamental cognitive operation through which each practice cycle produces expert development. Each successful deliberate practice cycle results in one of three chunk-level outcomes: (a) new chunk formation, (b) enrichment of an existing chunk, or (c) hierarchical integration of existing chunks. This explains why deliberate practice is more effective than routine experience: it specifically targets chunk construction at the boundary of current ability.
> **Source:** Section VI
> **Difficulty:** Advanced
> **Tags:** #original-synthesis #deliberate-practice #chunking

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[chunking-and-expertise-development-foundational-report-2026-04-15]])*
> **Q:** Concept: Constraint-Liberation Dialectic
> **A:** **Q:** What is the constraint-liberation dialectic described in this report?
> **A:** The paradox that working memory limitation is simultaneously (a) the obstacle that makes novice performance difficult and (b) the structural precondition that makes expertise possible. Without the constraint, there would be no adaptive pressure to organize information into chunks, and the rich hierarchical knowledge structures that constitute expertise would never develop. The constraint does not merely impede learning — it is the condition that drives it.
> **Source:** Section I, Synthesis
> **Difficulty:** Advanced
> **Tags:** #original-synthesis #working-memory #expertise-development

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[cognitive-load-theory-and-pkm-foundational-report-2026-04-12]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Question:** What is the core claim of CLT's prescription for instruction?
> **Answer:** Instructional design should minimize extraneous cognitive load (load generated by poor design) and ensure that intrinsic load is directed toward schema formation, because working memory capacity is severely limited and must not be wasted on processing that does not produce long-term memory schemas.
> **Source:** Section 2
> **Difficulty:** Basic
> **Tags:** CLT, working-memory, instruction

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[cognitive-load-theory-and-pkm-foundational-report-2026-04-12]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Question:** Distinguish intrinsic from extraneous cognitive load. Give a PKM example of each.
> **Answer:** Intrinsic load arises from the inherent complexity of the material (element interactivity); it cannot be eliminated without reducing content complexity. Extraneous load arises from poor design that does not contribute to schema formation; it can and should be minimized. PKM example of intrinsic: understanding a complex philosophical argument that requires holding many interrelated premises simultaneously. PKM example of extraneous: having to navigate between five different notes to assemble information that could be co-located in one atomic note.
> **Source:** Section 2
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** CLT, intrinsic-load, extraneous-load, PKM

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[cognitive-load-theory-and-pkm-foundational-report-2026-04-12]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Question:** What is the expertise-reversal effect, and what does it imply for PKM template design?
> **Answer:** The expertise-reversal effect is the phenomenon whereby instructional scaffolding that reduces cognitive load for novices becomes redundant and counterproductive load for experts who have internalized the relevant schemas. For PKM template design, it implies that scaffolding and elaboration that helps beginners structure their notes should be progressively reduced as the practitioner's expertise in a domain grows; static templates that never evolve will generate redundancy load for the expert practitioner.
> **Source:** Sections 3.5, 6
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** expertise-reversal, PKM, template-design

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[cognitive-load-theory-and-pkm-foundational-report-2026-04-12]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Question:** Define the split-attention effect and describe how atomic note design addresses it.
> **Answer:** The split-attention effect is the performance/learning decrement from having to simultaneously attend to and mentally integrate multiple spatially or temporally separated sources of mutually dependent information. Atomic note design addresses it by ensuring that each note is self-contained with respect to its central claim: all information necessary to understand the claim is co-located within the note, so the reader does not need to simultaneously consult multiple sources to understand any single claim.
> **Source:** Sections 3.2, 6
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** split-attention, atomic-notes, PKM

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[cognitive-load-theory-and-pkm-foundational-report-2026-04-12]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Question:** What is the Load Topology of PKM Workflows?
> **Answer:** An original framework (this report) that maps PKM workflow phases (Capture → Structure → Connect → Retrieve) to their dominant load type and risk, and the corresponding optimal load management strategy. Capture: risk of extraneous load from simultaneous transcription + comprehension; strategy: selective abstraction with deliberate delay. Structure: risk of intrinsic load from 과high element-interactivity notes; strategy: atomicity. Connect: risk of extraneous from automated/unannotated links; strategy: annotated self-explanation linking. Retrieve: risk of passive re-reading; strategy: spaced retrieval practice.
> **Source:** Section 5
> **Difficulty:** Advanced
> **Tags:** CLT, PKM, original-framework, load-topology

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[cognitive-load-theory-and-pkm-foundational-report-2026-04-12]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Question:** Why does the retrieval practice effect produce superior memory consolidation compared to passive re-reading, in CLT terms?
> **Answer:** Retrieval practice generates a distinct pattern of cognitive reactivation: reconstructing information from memory requires schema activation and application, generating the moderate-difficulty germane processing that consolidates memory traces. Passive re-reading merely re-exposes the brain to the same input without requiring reactivation of the stored schema, producing recognition without consolidation. In CLT terms, retrieval practice generates productive germane load (schema reactivation and strengthening) while passive re-reading generates low-benefit processing that mimics encoding without the consolidation-generating cognitive effort.
> **Source:** Section 5
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** retrieval-practice, germane-load, PKM, spaced-repetition

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[cognitive-load-theory-and-pkm-foundational-report-2026-04-12]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Question:** What is cognitive offloading, and when is it adaptive vs. maladaptive in PKM practice?
> **Answer:** Cognitive offloading is using external resources to perform cognitive operations that would otherwise be performed internally, reducing working memory demands. In PKM: adaptive when the offloaded operation (storing a reference, recording a fact for later retrieval) would impose excessive WM load without contributing to schema formation — the external storage frees WM for the schema-building processing that matters. Maladaptive when it substitutes for internal processing that produces durable schemas: using AI to summarize instead of reading and reformulating eliminates the germane processing that would have formed the schema.
> **Source:** Section 4
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** cognitive-offloading, PKM, schema-formation

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[cognitive-load-theory-and-pkm-foundational-report-2026-04-12]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Question:** What is the CLT-PKM Alignment Framework, and what are its four phases?
> **Answer:** An original two-dimensional framework (this report) integrating CLT effects with PKM workflow phases. Four phases: (1) Capture — risk: extraneous load from simultaneous tasks; mechanism: elaborative encoding through reformulation; strategy: selective capture + delayed processing. (2) Structure — risk: intrinsic load from high element-interactivity notes; mechanism: chunking through well-scoped note boundaries; strategy: atomicity. (3) Connect — risk: extraneous load from automated/unannotated links; mechanism: relational schema construction; strategy: annotated linking with self-explanation rationale. (4) Retrieve — risk: passive re-reading without consolidation; mechanism: schema strengthening through reactivation; strategy: spaced retrieval practice.
> **Source:** Section 5
> **Difficulty:** Advanced
> **Tags:** CLT-PKM-alignment, original-framework, PKM

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[cognitive-load-theory-and-pkm-foundational-report-2026-04-12]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Question:** What does the modality effect recommend for PKM note design, and what is its boundary condition?
> **Answer:** The modality effect recommends that PKM notes on complex, high-element-interactivity material should integrate verbal and visual representations (diagrams, concept maps, timelines) because verbal-visual integration distributes processing across two working memory subsystems (phonological loop + visuospatial sketchpad), effectively expanding available processing capacity. Boundary condition: the benefit holds only when both representations carry complementary (non-redundant) information. A diagram that merely illustrates what the adjacent text already states creates redundancy load rather than load-distribution benefit.
> **Source:** Sections 3.4, 6
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** modality-effect, dual-coding, PKM-design

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[cognitive-load-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-12]])*
> **Q:** What is Cognitive Load Theory?
> **A:** **Q:** What is Cognitive Load Theory, and what is its central claim?
> **A:** CLT is an instructional design theory arguing that learning is optimized when instructional procedures accommodate the limitations of human cognitive architecture — specifically, the limited capacity of working memory and the organizational role of schemas in long-term memory. Its central claim is that instructional design should manage cognitive load by reducing extraneous demands and directing processing toward schema construction.
> **Source:** Sections 1-2
> **Difficulty:** Basic
> **Tags:** #clt-definition, #instructional-design

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[cognitive-load-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-12]])*
> **Q:** Three Types of Cognitive Load
> **A:** **Q:** Name and define the three types of cognitive load in CLT's original framework.
> **A:** (1) *Intrinsic load* — inherent complexity of the material determined by element interactivity relative to learner expertise; cannot be altered by design. (2) *Extraneous load* — unnecessary load imposed by poor instructional design; always detrimental and always reducible. (3) *Germane load* — working memory resources devoted to schema construction; later reconceptualized as not an independent source but a functional description of resources allocated to processing intrinsic load.
> **Source:** Section 3
> **Difficulty:** Basic
> **Tags:** #load-types, #intrinsic, #extraneous, #germane

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[cognitive-load-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-12]])*
> **Q:** Element Interactivity
> **A:** **Q:** What is element interactivity, and why is it central to CLT?
> **A:** Element interactivity is the degree to which elements of a learning task must be processed simultaneously because they are logically interdependent. It is central because it determines intrinsic cognitive load: high interactivity means more elements must be held and integrated in working memory simultaneously. It is also expertise-relative — experts chunk elements via schemas, reducing effective interactivity.
> **Source:** Section 4
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** #element-interactivity, #intrinsic-load, #mechanism

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[cognitive-load-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-12]])*
> **Q:** Worked Example vs. Problem Solving
> **A:** **Q:** Why do worked examples produce better learning than problem-solving practice for novices, according to CLT?
> **A:** Conventional problem-solving requires means-ends analysis — simultaneously maintaining goal state, current state, operators, and subgoals — which imposes heavy extraneous load that does not contribute to schema construction. Worked examples eliminate means-ends analysis, allowing learners to devote working memory to understanding the solution rationale, which promotes schema construction.
> **Source:** Section 5.1
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** #worked-example-effect, #means-ends-analysis, #schema-construction

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[cognitive-load-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-12]])*
> **Q:** Expertise Reversal Effect
> **A:** **Q:** What is the expertise reversal effect, and what is its key implication for instructional design?
> **A:** The expertise reversal effect is the finding that instructional techniques effective for novices (e.g., worked examples, integrated formats) become ineffective or counterproductive for advanced learners. The key implication is that no single instructional approach is universally optimal — effective CLT-based design must be adaptive, adjusting support to learner expertise level.
> **Source:** Sections 4, 5.5
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** #expertise-reversal, #adaptive-instruction

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[cognitive-load-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-12]])*
> **Q:** Split Attention vs. Redundancy
> **A:** **Q:** What is the difference between the split attention effect and the redundancy effect?
> **A:** Split attention occurs when learners must mentally integrate two *mutually referring* sources that cannot be understood in isolation (solution: physically integrate them). Redundancy occurs when two sources are *self-contained* — each can be understood independently, so the additional source adds processing cost without information value (solution: remove the redundant source). The key distinction: mutually referring sources need integration; self-contained sources need elimination.
> **Source:** Sections 5.2, 5.3
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** #split-attention, #redundancy, #design-principle

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[cognitive-load-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-12]])*
> **Q:** Germane Load Reconceptualization
> **A:** **Q:** How did Sweller (2010) reconceptualize germane cognitive load, and why?
> **A:** Sweller argued that germane load is not an independent load source parallel to intrinsic and extraneous load, but rather the working memory resources devoted to processing intrinsic load when extraneous load has been minimized. The reason: germane load cannot be independently measured or manipulated apart from intrinsic load, making it unfalsifiable as a separate construct. The reconceptualized model has only two independent sources: intrinsic and extraneous.
> **Source:** Section 7.1
> **Difficulty:** Advanced
> **Tags:** #germane-load, #reconceptualization, #theoretical-debate

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[cognitive-load-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-12]])*
> **Q:** CLT and Desirable Difficulties
> **A:** **Q:** How is the apparent tension between CLT (reduce load) and desirable difficulties (add difficulty) resolved?
> **A:** Desirable difficulties increase *intrinsic* processing demands — they require deeper engagement with the material's inherent structure (e.g., retrieval practice forces schema reconstruction, interleaving forces discrimination). They do not add extraneous load. CLT predicts these should enhance learning when total load remains within working memory capacity, which the evidence confirms — with the caveat that desirable difficulties fail when intrinsic load is already high.
> **Source:** Section 7.2
> **Difficulty:** Advanced
> **Tags:** #desirable-difficulties, #reconciliation, #intrinsic-load

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[cognitive-load-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-12]])*
> **Q:** Productive Failure
> **A:** **Q:** What is productive failure, and how does it challenge and extend CLT?
> **A:** Productive failure (Kapur) involves learners first attempting problems without instruction (and failing), then receiving direct instruction. It often outperforms instruction-first approaches. It challenges CLT's prediction that novices should receive guidance first, but extends the theory by demonstrating that pre-instruction failure activates prior knowledge, reveals gaps, and creates "knowledge differentiation" that makes subsequent instruction more effective. It suggests CLT needs temporal framing — optimizing across learning sequences, not just individual events.
> **Source:** Section 7.3
> **Difficulty:** Advanced
> **Tags:** #productive-failure, #boundary-condition, #sequence-effects

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[cognitive-load-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-12]])*
> **Q:** Working Memory Capacity Estimates
> **A:** **Q:** What are the key estimates of working memory capacity relevant to CLT, and who proposed them?
> **A:** George Miller (1956) proposed "7 ± 2" items, but this includes rehearsed and chunked items. Nelson Cowan (2001) argued that *pure* working memory capacity — controlling for rehearsal and chunking — is approximately 4 chunks. CLT adopts the more conservative estimate, as it follows directly that high element interactivity (>4 novel interacting elements) will exceed capacity for novices.
> **Source:** Section 2.1
> **Difficulty:** Basic
> **Tags:** #working-memory, #capacity, #miller, #cowan

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[generative-learning-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-03]])*
> **Q:** Definition — Generative Learning Theory** | Q: What is the core thesis of Generative Learning Theory, and who developed it?
> **A:** Developed by Merlin Wittrock (1974), GLT proposes that learning is an active process through which the learner constructs meaning by generating connections between new information and prior knowledge through the processes of selection, organization, and integration (SOI). The learner does not record information but builds mental models through active cognitive construction.

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[generative-learning-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-03]])*
> **Q:** Definition — Generation Effect** | Q: What is the generation effect, and who first systematically documented it?
> **A:** The generation effect (Slamecka & Graf, 1978) is the empirical finding that generating information from minimal cues produces better memory retention than passively reading the same information. It is one of educational psychology's most replicated findings and provides direct empirical support for GLT's core claim about active versus passive encoding.

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[generative-learning-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-03]])*
> **Q:** Definition — Generativity Paradox** | Q: What is the generativity paradox, and how is it resolved?
> **A:** The apparent contradiction between GLT's prescription for active generation and CLT's warning that cognitive demand impairs learning. Resolved by distinguishing load types: generative activities increase *germane* load (schema-building effort), not extraneous load. The constraint is that total load must stay within working memory capacity — a constraint dependent on learner prior knowledge and the material's intrinsic complexity.

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[generative-learning-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-03]])*
> **Q:** Distinction — Note-Taking vs. Note-Making** | Q: How do GLT and the note-taking research distinguish note-taking from note-making, and which produces deeper learning?
> **A:** Note-taking = passive transcription, minimal cognitive reorganization. Note-making = active transformation — reorganizing, paraphrasing, connecting to prior knowledge, and generating one's own structures. GLT predicts and Mueller & Oppenheimer (2014) demonstrate that note-making (exemplified by longhand's forced condensation) produces superior conceptual learning because it requires the organizing and integrating processes that build mental models.

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[generative-learning-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-03]])*
> **Q:** Concept — Expertise Reversal Effect** | Q: What is the expertise reversal effect, and what does it imply for generative strategy prescription?
> **A:** The expertise reversal effect (Kalyuga et al., 2003): instructional methods optimal for novices may impair experts, and vice versa. For generative strategies, this implies that strategies highly effective for intermediate/advanced learners may impose unsupportable cognitive load on novices who lack the foundational schemas to make generation productive. No universal optimal strategy exists — only strategies optimal for a particular learner at a particular expertise stage.

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[generative-learning-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-03]])*
> **Q:** Concept — Productive Failure** | Q: What is productive failure and what learning mechanism does it exploit?
> **A:** Kapur's instructional design principle: structure learning so students attempt complex problems *before* formal instruction. The pre-instruction struggle (usually including incorrect attempts) is "productive" because it activates, differentiates, and organizes prior knowledge relevant to the target concept, creating a "prepared mind" for instruction by establishing what needs to be explained. Exploits GLT's selection-organization-integration mechanism at the level of prior knowledge preparation.

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[generative-learning-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-03]])*
> **Q:** Concept — Desirable Difficulties** | Q: What are desirable difficulties and why does subjective difficulty predict learning benefit?
> **A:** Bjork's concept: conditions that increase short-term effort and difficulty (spaced practice, interleaved practice, reduced feedback) improve long-term retention and transfer. Difficulty is "desirable" when it increases *germane* cognitive load — effort invested in schema construction — rather than extraneous load. Subjective difficulty often signals productive generative processing precisely because generation is cognitively demanding, and cognitive demand is the signature of schema-building work.

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[generative-learning-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-03]])*
> **Q:** Process — CTML's Three Assumptions** | Q: What are the three foundational assumptions of Mayer's Cognitive Theory of Multimedia Learning?
> **A:** (1) Dual Channel — humans have separate processing channels for verbal/auditory and visual/pictorial information; (2) Limited Capacity — each channel can process only a limited number of elements simultaneously; (3) Active Processing — meaningful learning requires active selection, organization into verbal and pictorial mental models, and integration of those models with each other and with prior knowledge. These map directly onto Wittrock's SOI model within Baddeley's architectural framework.

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[generative-learning-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-03]])*
> **Q:** Application — Self-Explanation Strategy** | Q: How should self-explanation be deployed as a study strategy, and what conditions maximize its benefit?
> **A:** Self-explain by pausing while studying worked examples or complex texts to verbalize (or write) *why* each step is valid, what principle it illustrates, and how it connects to prior knowledge. Maximize benefit by: (1) using for material with inferential gaps rather than fully explicit text; (2) building sufficient prior knowledge first (self-explanation requires something to integrate with); (3) comparing your explanation against the source to identify errors; (4) focusing on causal and structural relationships, not surface description.

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[generative-learning-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-03]])*
> **Q:** Connection — GLT and Retrieval Practice** | Q: How does retrieval practice implement GLT's generative learning principle?
> **A:** Retrieval practice (self-testing, spaced repetition, practice tests) is generatively superior to re-reading because retrieval requires the active reconstruction of information from memory — engaging the selection and integration processes that constitute generative encoding. Each retrieval attempt strengthens both the memory trace and the retrieval routes to it. GLT explains *why* the well-replicated testing effect works: retrieval is inherently a generative act, not a passive one.

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[generative-learning-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-03]])*
> **Q:** Distinction — Germane vs. Extraneous Cognitive Load** | Q: How do germane and extraneous cognitive load relate to GLT's prescription for generative activity?
> **A:** Extraneous load = effort wasted on processing irrelevant elements of the learning environment (bad design, split attention, redundancy). Germane load = effort directly invested in schema construction and automation. GLT promotes increasing germane load through generative strategies. CLT advises eliminating extraneous load to preserve working memory capacity for germane processing. The two frameworks complement when properly distinguished: both aim to maximize the proportion of available cognitive resources devoted to actual schema building.

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[generative-learning-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-03]])*
> **Q:** Concept — ICAP Hierarchy and Constructive Activities** | Q: What distinguishes Constructive from Active activities in Chi's ICAP framework, and why does this distinction matter for GLT?
> **A:** Active activities (highlighting, copying, underlining) engage the learner behaviorally but do not produce outputs that go beyond the presented information — they manipulate surface structure without genuinely transforming it. Constructive activities (summarizing, self-explaining, concept mapping, elaborating) require producing outputs that *add to* the presented information — generating inferences, connections, organizations, and elaborations not present in the source. This distinction matters for GLT because it identifies that the critical threshold for generative processing is the production of learner-generated content that exceeds the information encountered, not mere behavioral engagement with the material.

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[pkm-and-cognitive-load-foundational-report-2026-04-05]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Q:** What does Cognitive Load Theory identify as the primary constraint on learning?
> **A:** The limited capacity of working memory. Learning requires processing new information through working memory, which can hold approximately 4 independent chunks simultaneously. Instruction that overloads working memory prevents the schema formation that constitutes lasting learning.
> **Source:** Section 2 — CLT Foundations
> **Difficulty:** Basic
> **Tags:** #clt #working-memory #cognitive-architecture

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[pkm-and-cognitive-load-foundational-report-2026-04-05]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Q:** What is the additivity constraint in Cognitive Load Theory?
> **A:** The principle that intrinsic, extraneous, and germane loads sum to a total cognitive load that must not exceed working memory capacity. A design implication: if intrinsic load is high (complex material), extraneous load reduction becomes especially critical, as the capacity available for productive engagement is already substantially consumed.
> **Source:** Section 3 — Architecture of Cognitive Load
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** #clt #intrinsic-load #working-memory

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[pkm-and-cognitive-load-foundational-report-2026-04-05]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Q:** What distinguishes intrinsic from extraneous cognitive load?
> **A:** Intrinsic load arises from the inherent complexity of the material (element interactivity) and cannot be reduced without changing the material itself. Extraneous load arises from how the material is presented or organised and is always potentially reducible through better design. The diagnostic question: is the difficulty from the topic or from the structure?
> **Source:** Section 3 — Architecture of Cognitive Load
> **Difficulty:** Basic
> **Tags:** #clt #intrinsic-load #extraneous-load #distinction

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[pkm-and-cognitive-load-foundational-report-2026-04-05]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Q:** What is the expertise-reversal effect and why does it matter for PKM?
> **A:** The expertise-reversal effect is the phenomenon whereby instructional supports effective for novices (worked examples, detailed scaffolding, explicit procedures) become ineffective or counterproductive for experts. For PKM, it implies that optimal system design is expertise-relative: highly structured, prescriptive frameworks suit beginners; flexible, sparse systems with rich semantic linking suit advanced practitioners. There is no universally optimal PKM design.
> **Source:** Section 8 — Expertise-Reversal Effect
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** #expertise-reversal #pkm-design #adaptive-systems

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[pkm-and-cognitive-load-foundational-report-2026-04-05]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Q:** What is synthesis debt and why does it generate extraneous load?
> **A:** Synthesis debt is the accumulated backlog of captured but unprocessed information in a PKM system. It generates extraneous load primarily through incomplete-task intrusion: unclosed cognitive loops around material that has been stored but not evaluated. Additionally, future synthesis becomes more costly as context recedes — re-contextualising notes captured weeks or months ago requires more effort than immediate processing.
> **Source:** Section 5 — How PKM Systems Fail
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** #synthesis-debt #extraneous-load #pkm-failure-modes

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[pkm-and-cognitive-load-foundational-report-2026-04-05]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Q:** What is the episodic buffer and why is it significant for PKM?
> **A:** The episodic buffer is the fourth component of Baddeley's working memory model — a limited-capacity, multi-modal workspace that integrates information from the phonological loop, visuospatial sketchpad, and long-term memory into coherent episodes. It is significant for PKM because synthesis tasks — integrating information from multiple notes, connecting new material to existing schemas — appear to engage the episodic buffer as their primary workspace. PKM activities that preserve the multi-modal, integrated representations the episodic buffer generates are most supportive of synthesis.
> **Source:** Section 4 — Working Memory Architecture
> **Difficulty:** Advanced
> **Tags:** #episodic-buffer #working-memory #pkm-synthesis

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[pkm-and-cognitive-load-foundational-report-2026-04-05]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Q:** What is the generation effect and how should it inform PKM practice?
> **A:** The generation effect is the robust finding that self-generated items (solutions, examples, connects) are better retained than items received in completed form. For PKM practice, it implies that generating your own examples, questions, and summaries when processing notes produces stronger encoding than passively annotating or highlighting. This is a core rationale for write-from-memory protocols.
> **Source:** Section 7 — Germane Load and Schema Construction
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** #generation-effect #elaborative-encoding #pkm-practice

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[pkm-and-cognitive-load-foundational-report-2026-04-05]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Q:** What is the Cognitive Priority Principle?
> **A:** The Cognitive Priority Principle (proposed in this report) holds that PKM practitioners should allocate cognitive resources proportionally to their cognitive value. Synthesis and connection generation — the highest-value activities — should receive the most protected, focused cognitive effort. Capture and filing — the lowest-value activities — should be made as automatic and effortless as possible. The error is allocating to the PKM system's maintenance precisely the concentrated, deliberate effort that should be directed to synthesis.
> **Source:** Synthesis section — Cognitive Priority Principle
> **Difficulty:** Advanced
> **Tags:** #cognitive-priority-principle #pkm-design #original-synthesis

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[pkm-and-cognitive-load-foundational-report-2026-04-05]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Q:** What does research on retrieval practice imply for PKM design?
> **A:** Karpicke & Roediger (2008) demonstrated that a single retrieval test produces better long-term retention than four re-studying sessions. For PKM design, this implies that the dominant activity in a PKM review cycle should be active recall (generating content without looking at notes) rather than passive re-reading. Systems that only support capture and browsing, without building in retrieval practice mechanisms, fail to leverage the most effective consolidation strategy available.
> **Source:** Section 7 — Germane Load and Schema Construction
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** #retrieval-practice #spaced-repetition #pkm-design

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[pkm-and-cognitive-load-foundational-report-2026-04-05]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Q:** What are the three conditions under which a PKM system functions as cognitive infrastructure rather than cognitive overhead?
> **A:** (1) Access costs are lower than reconstruction costs for stored material — the system reliably surfaces relevant content in less time and cognitive effort than would be needed to reconstruct it internally. (2) Maintenance costs remain low enough that they do not consume working memory resources needed for synthesis — the system is largely self-maintaining or requires minimal ongoing curation. (3) The system actively promotes germane processing through its structural logic — it requires or rewards connection-making, retrieval, and elaboration rather than only rewarding accumulation.
> **Source:** Synthesis section
> **Difficulty:** Advanced
> **Tags:** #cognitive-infrastructure #pkm-design #cognitive-conditions

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[pkm-and-cognitive-load-foundational-report-2026-04-05]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Q:** What is element interactivity and how does it determine intrinsic load?
> **A:** Element interactivity is the degree to which understanding a body of information requires simultaneously processing multiple interdependent elements. High element interactivity = high intrinsic load. Knowledge management tasks with high element interactivity include: synthesising across multiple perspectives with conflicting claims, building models that integrate multiple constraints, and generating novel connections across domains. Tasks with low element interactivity include: tagging, filing, and labelling — but these generate extraneous rather than intrinsic load.
> **Source:** Section 3 and Section 6
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** #element-interactivity #intrinsic-load #knowledge-work
































































## Key Figures & Intellectual Lineage

> [!person] **Alan D. Baddeley (1934– )** *(from [[baddeley-model-of-working-memory-foundational-report-2026-04-03]])*
> **Alan Baddeley** is the founder and primary architect of the multicomponent working memory model. His 2000 review in *Nature Reviews Neuroscience* remains one of the most cited papers in cognitive psychology. Baddeley's work spans the entire developmental arc of the model from 1974 through the 2000 episodic buffer addition and subsequent empirical work. He served as Director of the Medical Research Council's Applied Psychology Unit (APU) in Cambridge, which produced many of the key empirical studies supporting the model. His broader research programme addresses amnesia, prospective memory, and the relationship between working memory and long-term memory consolidation.

> [!person] **Graham Hitch (1945– )** *(from [[baddeley-model-of-working-memory-foundational-report-2026-04-03]])*
> **Graham Hitch** co-developed the multicomponent model with Baddeley in the landmark 1974 paper. His sustained contributions to the empirical investigation of working memory — particularly the phonological loop's role in cognitive development and mathematical cognition — extended the model's scope across the lifespan. His later work with Christopher Jarrold on working memory and developmental disability has influenced understanding of intellectual developmental disorders.

> [!person] **Nelson Cowan (1951– )** *(from [[baddeley-model-of-working-memory-foundational-report-2026-04-03]])*
> **Nelson Cowan** developed the embedded-processes model of working memory as the primary theoretical alternative to Baddeley's multicomponent framework. His 2001 capacity estimate of ~4 chunks in the focus of attention has become a widely cited marker of working memory's fundamental limit. His work on the development of working memory in childhood (alongside colleagues including Candice Morey and Christopher Jarrold) has been particularly influential on developmental cognitive psychology.

> [!person] **John Sweller** *(from [[baddeley-model-of-working-memory-foundational-report-2026-04-03]])*
> **John Sweller** developed [[cognitive-load-theory]] explicitly from Baddeley's working memory architecture, first articulating the theory in his 1988 paper on cognitive load during problem-solving. His programme of research identified the split-attention effect, the worked-example effect, the modality effect, and many other instructional phenomena predicted by CLT, and his collaborations with Fred Paas, Paul Chandler, and others produced the extensive meta-analytic evidence base that CLT now rests on.

> [!person] **Richard Mayer (1947– )** *(from [[baddeley-model-of-working-memory-foundational-report-2026-04-03]])*
> **Richard Mayer** developed the [[cognitive-theory-of-multimedia-learning]] in parallel with CLT, drawing on Baddeley's dual-channel architecture to produce twelve multimedia design principles supported by a large body of controlled experimental evidence. His textbook *Multimedia Learning* (2001; 2nd ed. 2009) is one of the most widely used educational psychology references internationally. His research on the modality effect, coherence principle, and segmenting principle has directly informed e-learning design worldwide.

> [!person] **George A. Miller (1920–2012)** — Harvard University, Princeton University** *(from [[chunking-and-expertise-development-foundational-report-2026-04-15]])*
> Foundational contributor to the cognitive revolution and the study of memory capacity. Miller's 1956 paper "The Magical Number Seven, Plus or Minus Two" established the concept of chunking and the limited capacity of short-term memory, providing the intellectual starting point for the entire research tradition examined in this report. His work bridged [[Information-Theory|information theory]] and experimental psychology, demonstrating that human information processing could be analysed in terms of channel capacity and recoding.
> **Key works referenced:** Miller (1956). *The Magical Number Seven, Plus or Minus Two.*
> **Relationships:** Influenced Simon, Newell, and virtually all subsequent working memory and expertise researchers.

> [!person] **Herbert A. Simon (1916–2001)** — Carnegie Mellon University** *(from [[chunking-and-expertise-development-foundational-report-2026-04-15]])*
> Nobel laureate in economics, pioneer of artificial intelligence, and central figure in the study of expertise and bounded rationality. With William Chase, Simon conducted the landmark chess expertise studies that demonstrated the chunk-based nature of expert memory. With Fernand Gobet, he developed template theory. Simon's estimate of 50,000–100,000 chunks in expert memory and the ten-year rule of expertise remain foundational reference points.
> **Key works referenced:** Chase & Simon (1973); Simon & Gobet (1996).
> **Relationships:** Extended Miller's chunking concept into expertise research; influenced Ericsson, Sweller, and the entire expert-novice research paradigm.

> [!person] **K. Anders Ericsson (1947–2020)** — Florida State University** *(from [[chunking-and-expertise-development-foundational-report-2026-04-15]])*
> Developed the deliberate practice framework that has dominated expertise research since the 1990s. Ericsson argued that expert performance is primarily the product of extensive, targeted practice rather than innate talent, and that the mechanisms of expertise include the development of long-term working memory (LTWM) — domain-specific retrieval structures that allow experts to circumvent normal working memory limitations.
> **Key works referenced:** Ericsson, Krampe, & Tesch-Römer (1993); Ericsson & Pool (2016).
> **Relationships:** Built on Simon's chunk-based account of expertise; debated Hambrick and Macnamara on the practice-versus-talent question.

> [!person] **John Sweller (1946–)** — University of New South Wales** *(from [[chunking-and-expertise-development-foundational-report-2026-04-15]])*
> Developed [[cognitive-load-theory|cognitive load theory]], the most influential framework for translating the cognitive science of chunking and schema development into instructional design principles. Sweller's work on intrinsic, extraneous, and germane cognitive load provided the theoretical architecture for understanding how instruction should be calibrated to the learner's developing expertise.
> **Key works referenced:** Sweller (1988); Sweller, Ayres, & Kalyuga (2011).
> **Relationships:** Drew directly on Miller's capacity limits and Simon's chunking work; influenced van Merriënboer, Kalyuga, Paas.

> [!person] **Alan D. Baddeley (1934–)** — University of York** *(from [[chunking-and-expertise-development-foundational-report-2026-04-15]])*
> Developed the multicomponent model of [[working-memory|working memory]] (with Graham Hitch), which replaced the unitary short-term store of the Atkinson-Shiffrin model with a complex system comprising a central executive, phonological loop, visuospatial sketchpad, and episodic buffer. Baddeley's model provided the architectural framework within which chunking operates and helped clarify the distinction between passive storage and active manipulation.
> **Key works referenced:** Baddeley & Hitch (1974); Baddeley (2000).
> **Relationships:** Refined the working memory architecture within which Miller's chunking concept and Sweller's load theory operate.

> [!person] **Michelene T. H. Chi** — Arizona State University** *(from [[chunking-and-expertise-development-foundational-report-2026-04-15]])*
> Pioneered the study of expert-novice differences through her landmark studies of physics problem categorization, demonstrating that experts organize knowledge around deep structural principles while novices organize around surface features. This finding provided direct evidence that expertise involves the reorganization (not merely the accumulation) of domain knowledge.
> **Key works referenced:** Chi, Feltovich, & Glaser (1981).
> **Relationships:** Extended the expert-novice paradigm established by de Groot and Chase & Simon into the domain of problem categorization and conceptual understanding.

> [!person] **Gary Klein (1944–)** — Klein Associates** *(from [[chunking-and-expertise-development-foundational-report-2026-04-15]])*
> Developed the [[Recognition-Primed-Decision-Model|Recognition-Primed Decision]] model through studies of naturalistic expert decision making in high-stakes, time-pressured environments. Klein's work demonstrated that expert decisions are typically recognition-based rather than analytically comparative, providing the naturalistic decision-making complement to laboratory-based expertise research.
> **Key works referenced:** Klein (1998). *Sources of Power.*
> **Relationships:** Applied chunk-based models of expertise to real-world decision making; debated Kahneman on the conditions under which intuitive expertise is trustworthy.

> [!person] **Jeroen J. G. van Merriënboer** — Maastricht University** *(from [[chunking-and-expertise-development-foundational-report-2026-04-15]])*
> Developed the [[Four-Component-Instructional-Design|Four-Component Instructional Design]] (4C/ID) model, a comprehensive framework for designing instruction for complex learning tasks. The 4C/ID model translates the cognitive science of chunking, schema construction, and automation into a systematic instructional design methodology.
> **Key works referenced:** van Merriënboer & Kirschner (2018). *Ten Steps to Complex Learning.*
> **Relationships:** Built on Sweller's cognitive load theory; extended instructional design from simple to complex learning.

> [!person] **John Sweller (1946–)** *(from [[cognitive-load-theory-and-pkm-foundational-report-2026-04-12]])*
> Emeritus Professor, University of New South Wales, Australia. Developed Cognitive Load Theory beginning in 1988, originating the framework from research on problem-solving and the means-ends analysis strategy. Sweller's evolutionary psychology framing (biologically primary vs. secondary knowledge) provided CLT with its theoretical unity beyond empirical findings. His 2010 reconceptualization of germane load represented significant self-correction. CLT's application to instructional design is his primary legacy.

> [!person] **Fred Paas (1962–)** *(from [[cognitive-load-theory-and-pkm-foundational-report-2026-04-12]])*
> Professor, Erasmus University Rotterdam. Developed the Paas Cognitive Load Scale (1992), the most widely used measure of subjective mental effort in CLT research. Extensive work on self-assessment in learning, worked examples, and testing effects. Key collaborator with Sweller and van Merriënboer on the foundational CLT literature.

> [!person] **Jeroen van Merriënboer (1959–)** *(from [[cognitive-load-theory-and-pkm-foundational-report-2026-04-12]])*
> Professor, Maastricht University. Co-developer with Sweller and Paas of the CLT theoretical framework. Primary originator of the 4C/ID model (Four Component Instructional Design), the most systematic translation of CLT into practical instructional design methodology. Extensive work on complex learning, task authenticity, and whole-task practice.

> [!person] **Alan Baddeley (1934–)** *(from [[cognitive-load-theory-and-pkm-foundational-report-2026-04-12]])*
> Emeritus Professor, University of York, UK. Developed the multi-component model of working memory (1974, with Graham Hitch), which underwent major elaborations in 2000 (adding the episodic buffer). Baddeley's model is the working memory architecture that CLT implicitly adopts, providing the empirical basis for the phonological loop, visuospatial sketchpad, and central executive distinctions that inform multimodal instructional design.

> [!person] **Niklas Luhmann (1927–1998)** *(from [[cognitive-load-theory-and-pkm-foundational-report-2026-04-12]])*
> German sociologist and intellectual of extraordinary scope. Developed the Zettelkasten note-making method over a 40-year academic career producing over 70 books and 400 articles. Though not a learning scientist, Luhmann anticipated several CLT insights through his methodological practice: atomicity, explicit link annotation, distributed network rather than hierarchical organization. Luhmann's Zettelkasten is the empirical existence proof that an externalized associative network can amplify intellectual productivity in ways consistent with CLT's schema-formation requirements.

> [!person] **John Sweller (b. 1946) — University of New South Wales** *(from [[cognitive-load-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-12]])*
> Creator of Cognitive Load Theory. Sweller's foundational contribution was demonstrating experimentally that conventional problem-solving practice imposes higher cognitive load and produces less learning than studying worked examples (1985, 1988). He subsequently developed the theoretical framework linking cognitive architecture to instructional design, introduced element interactivity as the theory's central predictive construct, and most recently reconceptualized the three-factor model and introduced the evolutionary framing of biologically primary/secondary knowledge. His willingness to revise his own theory (the 2010 germane load reconceptualization) exemplifies intellectual honesty in theoretical development.
> 
> **Key works:** Sweller (1988), Sweller et al. (1998), Sweller (2010), Sweller (2011)

> [!person] **Fred Paas (b. 1963) — Erasmus University Rotterdam** *(from [[cognitive-load-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-12]])*
> Co-developer of the three-factor model and pioneer of cognitive load measurement methodology. [[fred-paas|Paas]] developed the widely used 9-point mental effort rating scale (1992) and co-authored the influential reformulation distinguishing intrinsic, extraneous, and germane load. His work on measurement has been foundational for empirical CLT research, even as the limitations of subjective measurement continue to challenge the field.
> 
> **Key works:** Paas (1992), Sweller, van Merriënboer & Paas (1998), Paas et al. (2003)

> [!person] **Jeroen van Merriënboer (b. 1959) — Maastricht University** *(from [[cognitive-load-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-12]])*
> Co-developer of the three-factor model and creator of the [[four-component-instructional-design-4cid|4C/ID (Four-Component Instructional Design)]] model. [[jeroen-van-merriënboer|Van Merriënboer]] translated CLT principles into a comprehensive instructional design methodology for complex learning environments, bridging the gap between theory and practice. He has also maintained the theoretical value of the germane load construct against Sweller's reconceptualization.
> 
> **Key works:** van Merriënboer (1997), Sweller, van Merriënboer & Paas (1998), van Merriënboer & Kirschner (2018)

> [!person] **Sergei Kalyuga — University of New South Wales** *(from [[cognitive-load-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-12]])*
> Principal researcher on the [[expertise-reversal-effect|expertise reversal effect]] and adaptive instruction. [[Sergei-Kalyuga|Kalyuga's]] work demonstrated that virtually all CLT effects reverse with increasing expertise, establishing one of the theory's most important boundary conditions and motivating the research program on adaptive CLT-based instruction.
> 
> **Key works:** Kalyuga et al. (2003), Kalyuga (2007), Kalyuga (2011)

> [!person] **Richard Mayer (b. 1947) — UC Santa Barbara** *(from [[cognitive-load-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-12]])*
> Developer of the [[cognitive-theory-of-multimedia-learning]], which independently derives many principles parallel to CLT effects from similar cognitive architecture assumptions. [[Richard-Mayer|Mayer's]] work on multimedia learning principles has provided the most extensive applied evidence base for CLT-adjacent design principles, and his research program has been instrumental in extending cognitive load considerations into technology-enhanced learning environments.
> 
> **Key works:** Mayer (2001, 2009), Mayer & Moreno (2003)

> [!person] **Paul Chandler — University of New South Wales (deceased)** *(from [[cognitive-load-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-12]])*
> Sweller's key early collaborator who co-developed the theoretical treatment of split attention and redundancy effects. Chandler's work was instrumental in establishing CLT's empirical effects catalog during the 1990s.
> 
> **Key works:** Chandler & Sweller (1991), Sweller & Chandler (1994)

> [!person] **Merlin Wittrock (1931–2007)** *(from [[generative-learning-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-03]])*
> Educational psychologist at UCLA, the originator of Generative Learning Theory. Wittrock's 1974 paper "A Generative Model of Mathematics Education" introduced the core thesis, which he subsequently elaborated and extended through the 1990s. His 1989 chapter "Generative Processes of Comprehension" is the canonical theoretical statement. Wittrock drew on Piaget's constructivism, cognitive science's information-processing revolution, and Rothkopf's mathemagenic concept to develop a uniquely learner-centered theory of cognitive engagement with educational content.

> [!person] **Richard Mayer (1947–)** *(from [[generative-learning-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-03]])*
> Educational psychologist at UC Santa Barbara and the primary architect of the Cognitive Theory of Multimedia Learning, which extends Wittrock's GLT to multimedia environments using Baddeley's dual-channel memory model. Mayer has authored over 500 publications and multiple landmark books including *Multimedia Learning* (2001, 2009), producing the most extensive empirical program in educational psychology's history. His CTML is the leading framework for evidence-based instructional design in multimedia contexts.

> [!person] **Michelene Chi (1950–)** *(from [[generative-learning-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-03]])*
> Cognitive scientist at Arizona State University whose research on self-explanation, knowledge revision, and the ICAP framework has profoundly extended GLT theory and application. Chi's 1989 paper documenting the self-explanation effect opened a major research program; her 2014 ICAP framework provided a comprehensive taxonomy of learning engagement levels.

> [!person] **Manu Kapur** *(from [[generative-learning-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-03]])*
> Educational psychologist whose research on productive failure challenged the conventional assumption that successful performance should precede independent practice. Kapur's experimental demonstrations that pre-instruction problem exploration improves subsequent learning provided an important counter-intuitive extension of generative learning principles to instructional sequencing.

> [!person] **John Sweller** (1946–present)** *(from [[pkm-and-cognitive-load-foundational-report-2026-04-05]])*
> Educational psychologist at the University of New South Wales; originator of Cognitive Load Theory (1988). Sweller's foundational insight came from studying problem-solving in mathematics: students using means-ends analysis performed worse on subsequent transfer tasks than students working through worked examples, because the problem-solving strategy saturated working memory with subgoal management rather than schema formation. From this observation, CLT developed.
> 
> **Core contribution to this report:** The three-load framework; the worked-example effect; the redundancy effect; the expertise-reversal reappraisal (with Kalyuga, 2019).
> 
> **Key works:** Sweller, J. (1988). *Cognitive load during problem solving*. *Cognitive Science, 12*(2), 257–285. Sweller, J., van Merriënboer, J. J. G., & Paas, F. (1998). *Cognitive architecture and instructional design*. *Educational Psychology Review, 10*, 251–296. Sweller, J., van Merriënboer, J. J. G., & Paas, F. (2019). Cognitive architecture and instructional design: 20 years later. *Educational Psychology Review, 31*, 261–292.

> [!person] **Alan Baddeley** (1934–present)** *(from [[pkm-and-cognitive-load-foundational-report-2026-04-05]])*
> British cognitive psychologist; developer (with Graham Hitch) of the influential multi-component model of working memory (1974), replacing Atkinson and Shiffrin's unitary short-term memory store with a three-then-four component architecture. Baddeley's model provides the cognitive architecture that CLT's load analysis operates within.
> 
> **Core contribution to this report:** Multi-component WM model (Central Executive, phonological loop, visuospatial sketchpad, episodic buffer); working memory capacity as the binding constraint on learning and complex cognition.

> [!person] **Fred Paas** (contemporary)** *(from [[pkm-and-cognitive-load-foundational-report-2026-04-05]])*
> Dutch educational psychologist; collaborator with Sweller; developer of the primary subjective measurement instrument for cognitive load — the 9-point mental effort rating scale (Paas, 1992). Paas's measurement methodology transformed CLT from a theoretical framework into an empirically testable research programme.
> 
> **Core contribution to this report:** Cognitive load measurement methodology; germane load operationalisation; systematic CLT research programme.

> [!person] **Jeroen van Merriënboer** (contemporary)** *(from [[pkm-and-cognitive-load-foundational-report-2026-04-05]])*
> Educational technologist at Maastricht University; collaborator with Sweller and Paas; architect of the [[Four-Component-Instructional-Design]] (4C/ID) model — an application of CLT principles to complex professional skills training. Van Merriënboer's work extends CLT from simple task learning to complex, whole-task skill development.
> 
> **Core contribution to this report:** Germane load concept (with Sweller and Paas, 1998); extension of CLT to complex learning; practical CLT design guidelines.

































## Open Questions

> [!open-question] **Is Working Memory Capacity Trainable?** *(from [[baddeley-model-of-working-memory-foundational-report-2026-04-03]])*
> The stakes are high: if working memory capacity is trainable, it could be a target for educational and clinical intervention. The evidence says: near-transfer gains (improvements on trained and similar tasks) are robust from working memory training programs. Far-transfer gains to untrained cognitive functions (reading comprehension, mathematics, intelligence) are not reliably produced by current training protocols. The theoretical question is whether this near-transfer specificity reflects a fundamental architectural limit (capacity is fixed and cannot be expanded, only redistributed) or a training methodology problem (current protocols are not the right kind of training to produce transfer). **Current Status**: Majority of meta-analytic evidence suggests limited far-transfer; the question remains theoretically open and practically important.

> [!open-question] **Working Memory and Consciousness** *(from [[baddeley-model-of-working-memory-foundational-report-2026-04-03]])*
> The episodic buffer's proposed connection to conscious awareness raises deep questions about the relationship between working memory and consciousness more broadly. Baars' Global Workspace Theory and Baddeley's working memory model share structural similarities — both involve a limited-capacity workspace that makes information globally available for cognitive processing, and both connect this availability to consciousness. Is working memory the cognitive architecture of consciousness? Or is working memory a functional architecture that sometimes, but not always, involves conscious awareness? **Current Status**: Philosophically alive, empirically difficult to resolve. The connection between working memory capacity and the stability of conscious representations (persistence of visual experience across eye movements, maintenance of auditory scenes) is an active research area.

> [!open-question] **The Neural Binding Problem for Chunks** *(from [[chunking-and-expertise-development-foundational-report-2026-04-15]])*
> How are the distributed neural representations that encode individual elements of a chunk bound together into a single retrievable unit? The cognitive account of chunking is well-developed, but the neural mechanism by which the brain implements chunk binding — creating stable, rapidly activatable neural assemblies from previously independent elements — remains incompletely understood. Candidate mechanisms include neural synchrony, Hebbian learning, and synaptic consolidation, but their specific roles in chunk formation have not been definitively established.

> [!open-question] **Open Question: What Is the Learning Curve for CLT-Informed PKM?** *(from [[cognitive-load-theory-and-pkm-foundational-report-2026-04-12]])*
> How long does it take for the cognitive benefits of a CLT-informed PKM design to exceed the cognitive costs of building and learning to use it? This question is unanswered in the literature. Anecdotal reports from Zettelkasten practitioners suggest a substantial initial investment period (months to years) before the system's network effects become genuinely productive. During this investment period, what CLT-based guidance would help practitioners persist and make good design decisions? This is an important practical research question.

> [!open-question] **The PKM-to-Learning Pathway: What Evidence Exists?** *(from [[cognitive-load-theory-and-pkm-foundational-report-2026-04-12]])*
> No randomized controlled study has measured the learning outcomes (retention, transfer, schema formation) of CLT-informed PKM design compared to CLT-naïve PKM design while holding content and time constant. The entire applied edifice of CLT + PKM rests on inferential extension of laboratory findings to naturalistic long-term knowledge management. This is a significant gap.

> [!open-question] **The Metacognitive Prerequisites for Effective CLT-Informed PKM** *(from [[cognitive-load-theory-and-pkm-foundational-report-2026-04-12]])*
> What level of metacognitive development is required before a CLT-informed PKM system produces net cognitive benefits? The system's efficiency gains may be outweighed by its maintenance demands for practitioners without well-developed [[metacognition|metacognitive monitoring skills]]. The learning curve for CLT-informed PKM is empirically unknown.

> [!open-question] **Does CLT need an affective/motivational extension?** *(from [[cognitive-load-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-12]])*
> CLT assumes that cognitive architecture alone determines learning outcomes, but motivation influences how much working memory capacity is actually deployed. A learner with low interest may not invest sufficient effort regardless of instructional design quality. Research on situational interest, self-determination, and flow suggests that affective states modulate the relationship between cognitive load and learning in ways CLT does not yet model. Whether this requires extending the theory or simply acknowledging a boundary condition remains debated.

> [!open-question] **Does Generative Learning Theory Apply Across Cultures?** *(from [[generative-learning-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-03]])*
> GLT was developed and primarily tested in Western, individualistic, academically oriented educational contexts. Whether the theory's prescriptions generalize across educational cultures that emphasize different relationships between teacher authority, student initiative, and knowledge construction — particularly collectivist cultures with strong transmission-model educational traditions — is an open empirical question. The cognitive mechanisms GLT describes (selection, organization, integration) are presumably universal features of human cognition; the instructional conditions that support or hinder them may be culturally variable.

> [!open-question] **The Long-Term Schema and PKM Evolution Question** *(from [[pkm-and-cognitive-load-foundational-report-2026-04-05]])*
> As a practitioner's schemas develop over years of PKM practice, does the system's role fundamentally change — from a cognitive extension that supplements limited internal storage to a cognitive mirror that reinforces and expands rich internal schemas? And if so, what design features support this evolution? No research has tracked the cognitive load profile of an individual PKM practitioner longitudinally across a decade or more of expertise development. The expertise-reversal principle predicts that such evidence would show a U-shaped relationship between system complexity and cognitive benefit: increasing complexity benefits novices up to a point, then reverses. But the location of the reversal point and the characteristics that predict it remain empirically uncharted.










## Protocols & Methods

> [!protocol] **Protocol: Designing Deliberate Practice for Chunk Construction** *(from [[chunking-and-expertise-development-foundational-report-2026-04-15]])*
> **Purpose:** Structure practice sessions to maximize the rate and quality of chunk formation.
> 
> **Steps:**
> 1. **Identify the target chunk gap.** Analyse current performance to determine what patterns the learner does not yet recognize automatically. Use expert models or diagnostic assessments to identify specific chunk-level deficiencies.
> 
> 2. **Design practice tasks at the chunk boundary.** Select tasks that contain both familiar and unfamiliar elements — familiar enough to activate existing chunks (reducing some load) but novel enough to require new chunk construction. This is the "zone of proximal chunk development."
> 
> 3. **Ensure immediate, informative feedback.** Arrange conditions so that the learner receives feedback on the accuracy and quality of their pattern recognition during or immediately after practice. Feedback should reference the relevant mental representation (standard of excellence).
> 
> 4. **Require effortful engagement.** The task must demand full concentration. Automated processing of already-formed chunks does not drive new chunk construction. If the task can be performed on "autopilot," it is too easy.
> 
> 5. **Iterate with progressive variation.** Repeat the practice cycle with variations that strengthen the chunk's robustness — different surface features with the same deep structure, different contexts that require the same pattern recognition, different response demands for the same situation perception.
> 
> 6. **Progressively withdraw scaffolding.** As new chunks form and consolidate, reduce the guidance and increase the complexity and autonomy of the practice tasks. Track the learner's developing chunk library and recalibrate continuously.

> [!protocol] **Protocol: Implementing a Faded Worked Example Sequence** *(from [[chunking-and-expertise-development-foundational-report-2026-04-15]])*
> **Purpose:** Guide learners from scaffolded example study to independent problem solving in a way that optimizes schema construction.
> 
> **Steps:**
> 1. **Present a fully worked example.** Show the complete solution with all steps explicitly displayed. Direct the learner to study the solution structure, not just the answer.
> 
> 2. **Present a partially worked example.** Remove one or two steps from the middle or end of the solution. Require the learner to generate the missing steps.
> 
> 3. **Increase the number of missing steps.** In subsequent examples, remove progressively more steps, always ensuring that the remaining steps provide enough scaffolding for the learner to infer the missing ones.
> 
> 4. **Present the problem only.** Once the learner can successfully complete examples with most steps removed, transition to unguided problem solving.
> 
> 5. **Monitor for the expertise reversal effect.** If the learner shows signs of being bored by, or having difficulty with, the worked examples (both can indicate that the examples have become redundant), accelerate the fading and move toward independent practice.

> [!protocol] **CLT-Informed PKM Capture Protocol** *(from [[cognitive-load-theory-and-pkm-foundational-report-2026-04-12]])*
> **Situation:** During active reading, lecture attendance, or research deep-dive.
> **Principle:** Separate comprehension (high intrinsic load) from capture elaboration (high germane demand) to prevent simultaneous load competition.
> 
> **Steps:**
> 1. During the learning event: capture only brief, sparse notes — key claims, surprising findings, confusing points, potential links. Phrase is fine. Full sentences not required. Goal: externalize enough to enable later processing, not to create a complete record.
> 2. Within 24 hours: return to the sparse notes in a dedicated processing session. For each sparse note, ask: (a) what is the single claim this represents? (b) how does it connect to what I already know? (c) what does it update or challenge in my existing schema?
> 3. Create permanent notes only during processing session, not during the learning event. Each permanent note: one claim, in your own words, linked to at least two related existing notes with brief annotation of the relationship.
> 4. Archive or delete sparse capture notes once processed; their function is working memory relief, not long-term documentation.
> 
> **CLT Rationale:** Steps 1-2 implement split-phase processing (avoiding simultaneous comprehension + synthesis load). Step 3 implements self-explanation through reformulation. Step 4 prevents note-accumulation without schema-formation.

> [!protocol] **Protocol: CLT-Based Material Redesign Audit** *(from [[cognitive-load-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-12]])*
> **Purpose:** Evaluate existing instructional materials for CLT compliance.
> 
> **Steps:**
> 1. **Identify the target learner's expertise level** — novice, intermediate, or advanced in this domain
> 2. **Estimate element interactivity** — how many concepts must be simultaneously processed?
> 3. **Scan for split attention** — are related information sources physically separated? If yes: physically integrate them
> 4. **Scan for redundancy** — are multiple sources saying the same thing? If yes: remove the redundant source
> 5. **Check modality** — can some visual text be converted to narration where diagrams are present?
> 6. **Assess scaffolding** — are worked examples provided before independent practice? Is there a fading sequence?
> 7. **Check for expertise reversal** — would this material be appropriate for all learners? If diverse audience: create adaptive pathways
> 8. **Evaluate sequencing** — are high-interactivity concepts preceded by their component elements?
> 
> **Output:** Prioritized list of CLT-informed redesign recommendations, ranked by expected impact.

> [!protocol] **Protocol: Worked Example Design Template** *(from [[cognitive-load-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-12]])*
> **Purpose:** Create CLT-optimized worked examples for novel instructional content.
> 
> **Steps:**
> 1. **Select a representative problem** from the target domain
> 2. **Solve it completely,** documenting every sub-step
> 3. **For each step, write an explanation** of *why* this step is taken (not just *what* is done)
> 4. **Integrate the explanation directly into the solution** (avoid split attention)
> 5. **Create a fading sequence:** Full example → partial example (2-3 steps removed) → more partial → full problem
> 6. **Add self-explanation prompts** at key decision points ("Why do you think we chose step X here?")
> 7. **Test with a novice learner;** if they struggle with the full example, reduce initial element interactivity by presenting prerequisite sub-problems first

> [!protocol] **Protocol 1: The Load Phase Audit** *(from [[pkm-and-cognitive-load-foundational-report-2026-04-05]])*
> A quarterly diagnostic to identify where cognitive load is being allocated in your PKM workflow.
> 
> **Step 1: Map your PKM activities to load phases.**
> List all activities you perform in operating your PKM system. For each, identify:
> - **Primary load type**: Extraneous (maintenance, navigation, filing decisions) / Intrinsic (comprehension, complex synthesis) / Germane (connection-making, elaboration, retrieval practice)
> - **Estimated weekly time investment**
> 
> **Step 2: Calculate load-type ratios.**
> Tally the weekly time in each category. Healthy ratios approximate:
